\title{Big-Data, Quality-Aware Forecasting of Intraoperative Hypotension Burden\\
from Beat-Synchronous Multimodal Hemodynamic Images}
\author{\IEEEauthorblockN{Tanner Boyle}
\IEEEauthorblockA{ESOF5052: Big Data Machine Learning\\Lakehead University\\
Instructor: Dr. T. Akilan \quad Teaching Assistant: Russell Frost}}

\begin{document}
\maketitle

\begin{abstract}
This report presents a quality-aware Beat-Synchronous Hemodynamic Image (BSHI) for forecasting intraoperative hypotension and its severity from arterial pressure (ART), electrocardiography (ECG), and optional photoplethysmography (PPG). Harmonized VitalDB and MOVER cohorts contain 52.7 million detected beats and 1.16 million eligible prediction windows. Analysis of waveform capture, signal quality, and hospital identifiability establishes the input selection and training protocol. The selected physiological context consists of five MAP-baseline variables and eight independently extracted ECG or ART parameters describing heart-rate variability, pulse morphology, pressure variability, and pressure drift. In the gradient-boosting feature comparison, the selected set achieved validation AUROC 0.8817 on pooled patients and 0.8058 for VitalDB-to-MOVER transfer, compared with 0.8684 and 0.7857 for the three-feature addition. A larger feature pool reached pooled AUROC 0.8864 but identified the hospital with AUROC 0.991. The optimizer comparison selected AdamW with second-moment decay 0.95, peak learning rate 0.0011, batch size 128, and cosine decay. The resulting BSHI specification combines a residual image encoder, a physiological context encoder, and per-signal quality weighting to forecast event risk, pressure drop, and hypotension burden. Source-only transfer and pooled patient-independent learning provide complementary evaluations of generalization, supported by a Parquet and Spark replay pipeline.
\end{abstract}

\begin{IEEEkeywords}
intraoperative hypotension, VitalDB, MOVER, beat-synchronous images, exploratory data analysis, signal quality, generalization, PySpark
\end{IEEEkeywords}

\section{Introduction}
Intraoperative hypotension (IOH) is associated with postoperative organ injury, and both the depth and duration of low mean arterial pressure (MAP) are clinically relevant \cite{ref1,ref2,ref5}. ART-waveform and multimodal prediction are established approaches \cite{ref3,ref6,ref9,ref12}. Adding signals, producing a spectrogram, or forecasting future pressure is therefore not sufficient as a research contribution \cite{ref10,ref25,ref26,ref35}.

This project examines whether organizing cardiovascular signals by cardiac cycle, retaining reliable morphology, and supplying a small amount of causal physiological context can improve forecasting across hospitals. The BSHI aligns consecutive beats while a parallel context encoder summarizes dynamics over one and five minutes. The outputs describe event probability, future MAP-drop magnitude, and the integrated burden below 65~mmHg, rather than only a binary alarm.

The analysis favors within-signal physiological context over cross-signal timing. Pulse delays can describe the monitor and recording system as much as the patient's condition. ECG and ART therefore define the primary model, PPG is optional, and the selected scalar parameters are calculated separately from each signal. This design balances discrimination, patient coverage, and resistance to hospital-specific acquisition effects.

\section{Datasets and Feasibility}
\textbf{VitalDB.} VitalDB, collected at Seoul National University Hospital, lists 6,388 surgical cases from 6,090 patients and provides high-resolution waveforms and numeric tracks \cite{ref16,ref21}. Of these, 3,458 surgeries contain ART, ECG lead II, PPG, and arterial-derived numeric MAP. Duration, waveform missingness, and MAP-density filters retain 3,185 surgeries from 3,085 patients. Waveforms are anti-aliased to a common 100~Hz grid; the selected recordings are essentially continuous and contain 12,089 waveform-hours.

\textbf{MOVER.} The Medical Informatics Operating Room Vitals and Events Repository contains operating-room records from the University of California, Irvine Medical Center \cite{refmover}. Its archives list 64,354 surgeries; 1,796 contain all three waveform modalities and 748 also have an invasive arterial line. Native waveform rates vary by channel, approximately 100 to 300~Hz, and capture is intermittent. After harmonization to 100~Hz, missing intervals remain missing. Window-level eligibility retains 420 surgeries from 405 patients and 2,669 waveform-hours. A surgery must provide at least 30 minutes of analyzable windows with at least 75\% valid samples under the selected cohort rule. MOVER is both the target hospital for transfer assessment and a source in the pooled experiment.

All surgeries from one patient remain in the same partition. The harmonized cohort contains 3,490 patients before beat-level restrictions. The primary ECG+ART cohort contains 904,741 windows; the complete three-waveform subset contains 795,759. These denominators are different from the broader label-eligible cohort and are kept separate throughout the report. EEG/BIS, ETCO$_2$, and ten-minute forecasting remain optional extensions rather than requirements for the primary analysis.

\section{Literature Review and Research Gap}
ART-based IOH prediction is well established \cite{ref3,ref6}. Multimodal work combines ECG, PPG, EEG/BIS, and other measurements \cite{ref9,ref12,ref28,ref29}; frequency-domain models and continuous pressure forecasting provide further strong comparators \cite{ref25,ref26,ref35}. Signal-quality estimation and cross-channel artifact assessment are also established \cite{ref17,ref19}. ECG and PPG fusion maps, physiological signal images, and pulse-arrival methods motivate technically feasible representations but do not by themselves demonstrate transferable IOH information \cite{ref31,ref38,ref40,ref41}. Evaluation design, selection bias, and the MAP gray zone are central concerns \cite{ref13,ref14,ref24}.

The contribution being tested is therefore a measured integration: beat-synchronous waveforms, explicitly selected single-signal context, reliability-aware fusion, and direct burden forecasting, evaluated against a strong MAP baseline at two hospitals. Neither ART variability nor external validation is claimed as a new concept. The EDA supplies the evidence for choosing the inputs and the experiments needed to test their value.

\section{Research Questions}
\textbf{RQ1: Representation.} Does BSHI with the selected context improve IOH forecasting over MAP, ART-only, raw multimodal, and time-frequency baselines on identical windows?

\textbf{RQ2: Severity.} Can the same representation predict IOH occurrence, MAP-drop magnitude, and future AUT65 burden without sacrificing event discrimination?

\textbf{RQ3: Reliability.} Does per-signal SQI weighting improve performance when a modality is noisy or missing, and does that benefit transfer across hospitals?

\textbf{RQ4: Scalability.} Can preprocessing and continuous replay process the waveform cohort efficiently, with measured throughput, storage, and latency?

\textbf{RQ5: Generalization.} Do the selected parameters support VitalDB-to-MOVER transfer as well as patient-independent pooled learning, and which gains survive the removal of hospital-specific features?

\section{Methodology}
Figure~\ref{fig:methodology} summarizes the selected design. The waveform representation, outcome definition, physiological context, and evaluation partitions form one reproducible pipeline. The methodology combines the evidence from cohort characterization, parameter selection, and neural optimization into a fixed model specification.

\subsection{Forecasting Setup}
\label{sec:forecast}
Predictions are issued every 30~s using a 90-second waveform observation interval and a five-minute forecasting horizon, $H=300$~s. The BSHI uses the 60 most recent completed cardiac cycles. Scalar lookbacks extend to 300~s where sufficient causal history exists; no samples after the prediction time are used. Native feature extraction retains a one-second beat-completion margin.

The primary event is sustained MAP below 65~mmHg for at least 60~s, bridging only the configured short gaps of at most 10~s. Windows already hypotensive in the preceding 60~s are excluded from onset prediction. Coverage and gap checks determine whether future labels are observed, and unavailable burden targets remain masked. Current MAP from 65 to 75~mmHg defines the retained clinical gray zone. The outputs are
\begin{align}
y_{\mathrm{event}} &= \mathbb{I}\{\text{sustained MAP}<65\text{ within }H\},\\
\Delta\mathrm{MAP} &= \max\!\left(0,\mathrm{MAP}_0-\min_{t\in(0,H]}\mathrm{MAP}(t)\right),\\
\mathrm{AUT}_{65} &= \frac{1}{60}\int_0^H \max\!\left(0,65-\mathrm{MAP}(t)\right)\,dt.
\end{align}
The factor $1/60$ expresses AUT65 in mmHg$\cdot$min when time is measured in seconds. Future MAP is used only to construct outcomes. Because a complete 60-second event must fit within the horizon, the farthest evaluated onset bin ends at 240~s, not 300~s.

\subsection{Selected Waveform, Morphology, and Quality Inputs}
ECG R peaks define the image's cardiac-phase axis. Each of 60 cycles is resampled to 200 phase samples, producing a $60\times200$ array per waveform. ECG and ART are the primary modalities. ECG is robustly standardized within the observed window; ART retains absolute pressure through fixed physical scaling. Beat masks remain explicit, and unusable beats are not interpolated across capture gaps.

Within-signal ART morphology, including pulse pressure, maximum upstroke, systolic duration, and area, is retained as a feature group. Per-signal SQIs weight the usable modalities. ECG RR intervals are valid within-signal timing information. In contrast, ECG-to-ART and ECG-to-PPG transit times, ART-to-PPG delays, ART and PPG correlation, and cross-signal SQI are excluded from the primary input recipe. Removing a group must remove its image band and its context summary, so it cannot re-enter through another branch.

The stored seven-plane layout can be retained for controlled experiments by masking the unused bands. This does not make the entire image pipeline single-signal: ART and PPG waveform cycles remain aligned to ECG. The independence constraint applies to the selected scalar parameters, whose beat detection and measurement use their own signal only.

PPG is evaluated as an ablation on the identical PPG-available patients and windows used by the ECG+ART comparator. It is not a requirement for inclusion in the main cohort. Raw PPG amplitude in device units is not used as a scalar predictor; the optional waveform and morphology branch uses within-window normalization.

\subsection{Final Scalar Parameter Specification}
\label{sec:parameters}
The final training specification contains eight engineered ECG or ART parameters in Table~\ref{tab:parameters}, plus five mandatory MAP-baseline variables: current MAP and its 60-second mean, minimum, standard deviation, and slope. Thus the selected numerical context contains \textbf{13 physiological values}, not eight total input channels.

\begin{table*}[!t]
\caption{Selected engineered context for the primary BSHI experiment. All eight values use causal within-case history and are added to the five MAP-baseline variables. ART pulse mean pressure is calculated from the waveform; it is not necessarily identical to the monitor's numeric MAP.}
\label{tab:parameters}
\centering\small
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{5pt}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}p{3.5cm}p{1.15cm}p{1.2cm}Y@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter identifier} & \textbf{Signal} & \textbf{History} & \textbf{Definition and selection rationale}\\
\midrule
\code{e_sdnn_l300} & ECG & 300 s & Standard deviation of normal RR intervals, in ms. Supplies longer-horizon heart-rate variability beyond the latest image beats.\\
\code{a_map_slope_l60} & ART & 60 s & Trend of beat-wise pulse mean pressure, in mmHg/min. Captures the recent pressure direction.\\
\code{a_dpdt_min_l60} & ART & 60 s & Median of the minimum post-peak dP/dt of each pulse, in mmHg/s. Describes the falling arterial pulse rather than another average pressure.\\
\code{a_map_sd_l300} & ART & 300 s & Standard deviation of pulse mean pressure, in mmHg. Measures longer-term instability.\\
\code{a_map_sd_l60} & ART & 60 s & The corresponding short-term variability. Retains recent information when long history is unavailable.\\
\code{a_pp_cv_l300} & ART & 300 s & Pulse-pressure standard deviation divided by mean pulse pressure. Measures relative pulse-amplitude variability.\\
\code{a_map_d60_300} & ART & 60, 300 s & Difference between recent and five-minute pulse-mean-pressure medians, in mmHg. Describes drift relative to the patient's own history.\\
\code{a_map_slope_l300} & ART & 300 s & Five-minute pulse-mean-pressure trend, in mmHg/min. Distinguishes sustained drift from a brief fluctuation.\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table*}

The neural context comprises these 13 physiological values and eight availability indicators, giving a \textbf{21-value context vector}. It replaces the 25-value context used in the neural input-recipe comparison, removing cross-signal summaries from the primary model. The five MAP-baseline variables remain required. Missing engineered values are imputed and standardized using training patients only, with availability flags preserved. The joint contribution of the values and their flags is assessed through domain classification and a missing-history ablation.

The short- and long-history pressure variables are selected as a bundle. Their complementary roles cover recent pressure direction, sustained drift, and instability relative to the patient's own history. The primary model uses all eight engineered values; a smaller context containing the original three plus five-minute robust pressure range provides a compactness ablation.

\subsection{Network and Training Objective}
A compact residual CNN encodes the beat image, while an MLP encodes the 21-value context. Group normalization avoids batch-dependent normalization statistics. The approximately 2.85-million-parameter network combines these encoders through a shared fusion layer and a quality gate. Input dimensions, normalization statistics, and active feature masks are versioned with the model checkpoint.

The multi-task objective is
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{event}}+0.3\mathcal{L}_{\Delta\mathrm{MAP}}+0.3\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{AUT}}.
\end{equation}
Event risk uses binary cross-entropy. Pressure drop uses Huber loss on a training-standardized target. AUT65 uses a two-part loss: occurrence of nonzero burden plus Huber loss on its standardized $\log(1+\mathrm{AUT}_{65})$ magnitude, with the magnitude loss applied only when burden is positive and observed. The auxiliary loss weights are fixed at 0.3. A classification-only ablation isolates the contribution of the pressure-drop and burden objectives to event discrimination.

\subsection{Comparison Models and Evaluation}
The comparison includes the five-variable MAP baseline, ART-only, raw multimodal and time-frequency models, and matched BSHI variants. For the selected design, the key ablation sequence is BSHI without added scalar context, BSHI with the three initially selected parameters, and BSHI with all eight selected parameters. The SQI gate and optional PPG branch are then varied separately. Input dimensions, active groups, and patient eligibility are recorded for every run.

AUROC measures ranking across thresholds, while AUPRC is a necessary companion at approximately 3\% prevalence. Evaluation also includes calibration slope and intercept, Brier score, event sensitivity, lead time, and false alarms/hour. MAP-drop and AUT65 outputs use MAE and RMSE with explicit target masks. Dense time-ordered replay preserves the gray zone and difficult negatives rather than selecting only easily separated examples \cite{ref13,ref14}. Patient-cluster resampling is used for uncertainty because overlapping windows are not independent clinical observations.

\section{Exploratory Data Analysis}
\label{sec:eda}
The EDA connects cohort feasibility, signal behavior, parameter selection, and neural optimization. Cohort statistics describe the available population; fixed-baseline evaluations establish discrimination across sites; gradient-boosting comparisons select the physiological context; and neural experiments determine the optimization configuration and input-recipe behavior.

\subsection{Cohort Construction and Data Description}
Table~\ref{tab:datasets} and Fig.~\ref{fig:funnel} summarize cohort construction. The selected surgeries contain 14,758 waveform-hours and approximately 52.7 million detected beats. Of 1,163,278 label-eligible windows, 38,251 are positive onset windows, corresponding to approximately 7,581 distinct hypotension episodes. Positive-window counts therefore must not be interpreted as independent events.

\subsection{Signal Capture and Quality}
VitalDB is predominantly continuous, whereas MOVER capture is intermittent. In the 24-case-per-site gap sample, MOVER's median uninterrupted valid run was 6~s and its median gap was 3~s; approximately 58\% of samples were valid. Only 59.5\% of MOVER waveform time fell within analyzable windows, compared with 87\% in VitalDB. Median ECG SQI was lower in MOVER, approximately 0.88 versus 0.94, while arterial quality was high at both sites.

Instrumentation differs as well. Median ECG-to-ART delay was approximately 0.154~s in MOVER and 0.180~s in VitalDB. Median ECG-to-PPG delay was 0.44 versus 0.65~s, and 94 MOVER cases had implausibly delayed PPG for more than half their beats. PPG amplitude was in device-native units, with means approximately 590 and 27. These are reasons to avoid raw amplitude and transit-time shortcuts, not evidence that every PPG-derived parameter is hospital-specific. Sixty-eight VitalDB cases with nonfunctional ECG were excluded from beat-based analyses but retained where valid for MAP-only descriptions.

Figure~\ref{fig:quality} compares the full distributions rather than only their medians. The concentration of high ART quality contrasts with the broader ECG-quality and PPG-delay distributions in MOVER.

\par\smallskip\pgfplotslegendfromname{qualitylegend}
\caption{Per-case signal distributions for VitalDB and MOVER. Each panel uses common bins across hospitals; density is per unit of the horizontal axis. The upper panels show heart rate and pulse-delay behavior, while the lower panels show modality quality and usable-beat coverage. Lower ECG quality and the shifted PPG delay in MOVER motivate explicit quality weighting and the removal of cross-signal delays from the primary model.}
\label{fig:quality}
\end{figure*}

\subsection{Prediction Windows, Labels, and Splits}
A prediction window requires adequate waveform validity and a recent numeric MAP, no older than 10~s. Positive-window prevalence is 3.08\% in VitalDB and 5.74\% in MOVER. The median onset lead time is approximately 128 and 126~s, respectively. MAP-drop distributions are right-skewed, and AUT65 is zero in 85.6\% of VitalDB and 77.4\% of MOVER windows, motivating the two-part burden target rather than ordinary regression on all windows.

The pooled patient allocation was approximately 70/15/15 (Table~\ref{tab:splits}). Beat and label restrictions reduce the development cohort to 638,827 training windows from 2,366 patients and 135,979 validation windows from 508 patients. The latter includes 9,479 MOVER windows from 65 patients, with 406 positive windows. A separate within-dataset split also exists, but its labels are not interchangeable with the pooled split. A patient can be training in one split definition and test in the other; clean comparisons must use one definition consistently.

\begin{table}[!t]
\caption{Pooled splits in the broad label-eligible cohort, before core restrictions. Positive windows overlap in time.}
\label{tab:splits}

\caption{Outcome distributions in the broad eligible cohort. Prevalence uses the original within-dataset patient splits. Event onsets span the evaluable lead-time interval; MAP-drop density is displayed up to 60~mmHg. AUT65 density includes only windows with nonzero burden, complementing the two-part burden objective.}
\label{fig:labels}
\end{figure*}

\subsection{Beat-Synchronous Images and Available Cohorts}
The ECG+ART core provides 904,741 windows and 28,278 positive windows; MOVER contributes 50,044 and 2,381. Adding the PPG usability requirement leaves 795,759 windows and 24,573 positives, including 31,995 and 1,206 in MOVER. One core window has missing MAP baseline information, leaving 904,740 windows for the independent-parameter analyses. Missing-history windows are retained when the remaining inputs are valid.

Native extraction produced 157 parameters and quality/coverage fields across 3,493 cases with no failed cases. Each signal used its own detections, segmentation, and causal history. Native arterial SBP, DBP, pulse pressure, and pulse-mean-pressure medians agreed with the existing beat store to median absolute differences of approximately 0.24 to 0.44~mmHg. Heart rate obtained independently from ECG, ART, and PPG was almost identical, with Spearman correlation at least 0.998. Single-signal extraction therefore does not imply statistical independence between physiological quantities.

Figures~\ref{fig:bshi-vitaldb} and~\ref{fig:bshi-mover} show recorded event windows from the two hospitals. The examples distinguish waveform morphology from derived context and quality bands in the seven-plane representation.

The waveform panels retain the observed cardiac-phase structure; three adjacent beats and four phase samples are averaged only for vector display. The training tensor remains $60\times200$. ECG/PPG use robust z-scores, ART uses $(P-80)/40$, derived bands use within-window z-scores, and SQI remains on $[0,1]$. Timing bands are ECG/ART, ECG/PPG, ART/PPG delays, and RR; morphology bands are pulse pressure, maximum dP/dt, systolic duration, ART area, PPG amplitude, AC/DC, upstroke, and ART/PPG correlation. The primary recipe masks the cross-signal timing, correlation, and SQI bands.}
\label{fig:bshi-vitaldb}
\end{figure*}

\caption{MOVER BSHI event window: current MAP 77~mmHg, onset 233~s ahead, and MAP drop 16~mmHg. The waveform panels retain the observed cardiac-phase structure; three adjacent beats and four phase samples are averaged only for vector display. The training tensor remains $60\times200$. ECG/PPG use robust z-scores, ART uses $(P-80)/40$, derived bands use within-window z-scores, and SQI remains on $[0,1]$. Timing bands are ECG/ART, ECG/PPG, ART/PPG delays, and RR; morphology bands are pulse pressure, maximum dP/dt, systolic duration, ART area, PPG amplitude, AC/DC, upstroke, and ART/PPG correlation. The primary recipe masks the cross-signal timing, correlation, and SQI bands.}
\label{fig:bshi-mover}
\end{figure*}



\subsection{Baseline Predictability and Cross-Site Behavior}
Fixed-baseline evaluations established that current pressure is a strong comparator (Table~\ref{tab:historical}). On identical PPG-available pooled test windows, five MAP variables achieved boosting AUROC 0.868, while the old complete feature set achieved 0.883. This modest pooled improvement did not persist in the broad VitalDB-to-MOVER comparison, where the complete set scored 0.843 versus 0.849 for MAP alone. The old feature set included morphology, HRV, cross-signal timing, PPG features, and quality summaries.

\begin{table*}[!t]
\caption{Fixed-baseline AUROC on PPG-available windows. The old complete feature set combines ART morphology, HRV, cross-signal timing, PPG, and quality summaries. These test evaluations establish the comparison baseline; parameter selection is reported separately on development partitions. Broad transfer settings use their original within-dataset split definitions.}
\label{tab:historical}
\centering\small
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}Yrrr@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Training and evaluation setting} & \textbf{Windows} & \textbf{MAP boosting} & \textbf{Old full-feature boosting}\\
\midrule
Pooled training, pooled test & 113,744 & 0.868 & 0.883\\
VitalDB training, all MOVER & 31,995 & 0.849 & 0.843\\
MOVER training, VitalDB test & 116,146 & 0.862 & 0.844\\
Pooled training, MOVER test subset & 5,611 & 0.891 & 0.890\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table*}

Performance also depends on difficulty. Near-onset windows are easier than distant onsets; in the fixed-baseline analyses, pooled AUROC fell from approximately 0.96 within 60~s to approximately 0.79 for MAP boosting at 180 to 240~s. A large share of positive windows arises in the 65 to 75~mmHg gray zone. Neither removing these windows nor emphasizing imminent events would demonstrate an improvement in the same forecasting problem.

Figures~\ref{fig:ladder} and~\ref{fig:roc} separate feature gains, learner capacity, calibration, and forecast difficulty. They retain the original fixed-baseline evaluation settings.

\begin{figure*}[!tp]
\centering

\subsection{Parameter Screening and Selection}
The initial catalogue contained 147 forecasting candidates: 88 ART, 32 ECG, and 27 PPG parameters. Training-only screening considered coverage, partial association beyond the five MAP variables, patient-grouped incremental discrimination, transfer direction, hospital identifiability, and redundancy. Coverage required at least 90\% overall and 85\% in MOVER. Domain AUROC above 0.70 or strong redundancy with current MAP restricted eligibility. Of six feasible candidates, forward selection retained ECG SDNN, one-minute native ART pressure slope, and minimum post-peak dP/dt as the compact three-parameter addition.

The missing-data analysis examined an important limitation of completeness-only screening. Five-minute ART variability had approximately 83.6\% MOVER coverage, just below the 85\% gate, despite substantial training-only incremental information. Missing-value-aware feature bundles retained those measurements without discarding patients. Histogram gradient boosting routed native missing values directly, and all compared models used the same core windows.

Within pooled training, 20\% of patients were held out for an inner check, stratified by hospital and presence of a positive window. Every third window within each case was used for fitting and this inner check. Fifteen feature sets and four training options were compared, then the shortlist was fixed before scoring all outer validation windows. The standard booster used 150 iterations, learning rate 0.1, 31 leaves, minimum leaf size 200, and L2 regularization 1, with fixed iterations rather than random window-level early stopping. The same inner design trained a separate model on VitalDB patients alone and checked held-out MOVER training patients.

\subsection{Matched Validation Results}
Table~\ref{tab:selection} and Fig.~\ref{fig:selection} give the matched results. The selected eight-parameter addition improves pooled validation AUROC from 0.8684 to 0.8817 compared with the three-parameter addition, and source-only MOVER validation from 0.7857 to 0.8058. Paired patient-cluster bootstrap improvements are $+0.0133$ [0.0082, 0.0175] and $+0.0200$ [0.0081, 0.0313], respectively. Pooled AUPRC increases from 0.2416 to 0.2707, and transfer AUPRC from 0.1755 to 0.2180.

\caption{Gradient-boosting feature comparison with 95\% patient-cluster bootstrap intervals from 200 resamples. Green identifies the selected eight-feature addition, giving 13 physiological values including the MAP baseline. The larger models use 147 values. Each panel evaluates identical windows, exposing the trade-off between pooled discrimination and hospital transfer.}
\label{fig:selection}
\end{figure*}

Relative to the five-variable MAP baseline, the selected context improves pooled AUROC by $+0.0224$ [0.0175, 0.0280]. The transfer difference is $+0.0085$ [$-0.0140$, 0.0308], with AUPRC 0.2180 versus 0.2241 for MAP alone. The selection therefore rests on the combined evidence: a clear pooled gain, improved transfer over the three-feature set, and substantially lower hospital identifiability than the large feature pool.

History availability remains clinically important. In MOVER windows with five-minute ART history, the selected context achieves source-only AUROC 0.8285 versus 0.8018 for MAP alone. Without that history, the corresponding values are 0.7349 and 0.7690. The model therefore retains short-history measurements and explicit availability indicators, and evaluation reports missing-history windows as a separate subgroup. A short-history fallback is included in the context ablation rather than removing these patients from the cohort.

\subsection{Dataset Shift and the PPG Decision}
The large feature set, despite selecting individually low-domain parameters, identifies the hospital with AUROC 0.991 on held-out training patients. The selected compact set has domain AUROC 0.761, close to 0.753 for the three-parameter addition and substantially below the large set. These values do not imply complete invariance: ECG variability and missing-history patterns still carry site information. Univariate domain screening alone is not sufficient because a combination of individually innocuous measurements can identify the acquisition system.

Requiring PPG removes 36.1\% of MOVER core windows and 49.3\% of its positive windows, compared with 10.6\% and 9.8\% in VitalDB (Fig.~\ref{fig:shift}). Optional PPG features did not provide consistent transferable value. PPG is therefore excluded from the primary model and retained as a normalized-waveform ablation on matched windows. This preserves the main cohort while directly measuring any incremental benefit of the third modality.

\subsection{Optimizer and Learning-Rate Selection}
\label{sec:optimizer}
Training choices were based on development patients, not on the tabular test results. The approximately 2.85-million-parameter BSHI encoder served as the neural test bed. A 300-step learning-rate range test increased the rate exponentially while tracking smoothed loss \cite{refsmith}. AdamW minima occurred near 0.011, but instability appeared above approximately 0.06. Candidate AdamW peaks were therefore 0.00037, 0.0011, and 0.0037. SGD with Nesterov momentum was checked at 0.0018, 0.0053, and 0.018 \cite{refadamw,refsutskever}.

Nine optimizer/rate combinations used up to six short epochs of 29,000 windows on a 58,000-window training and 19,000-window validation subset. Validation AUPRC was weighted back to natural prevalence and was the primary selection metric; AUROC was a tie-breaker. AdamW with $\beta_2=0.95$ at 0.0011 achieved AUPRC 0.2491, while the best default-$\beta_2$ AdamW run achieved 0.2475. The strongest SGD run reached only 0.2127 (Fig.~\ref{fig:opt}). The largest rate for $\beta_2=0.95$ stopped early, supporting a moderate learning rate rather than simply faster updates.

Four local configuration checks varied batch size, weight decay, and the learning-rate schedule. The best three were repeated with seed 5053 as well as seed 5052, for 16 short trials in total. Batch 128 achieved mean AUPRC 0.2540 and AUROC 0.8805; the corresponding means for batch 256 and the one-cycle schedule are shown in Table~\ref{tab:opt}. Batch 128 is selected for its highest two-seed mean AUPRC and lower memory demand than larger batches. The configuration combines strong discrimination with a practical training footprint.

The final training configuration is AdamW, $\beta_2=0.95$, peak learning rate 0.0011, weight decay 0.01, batch size 128, one warm-up epoch, and cosine decay. Training uses at most 30 epochs, stopping on validation AUPRC with patience 5 and minimum improvement 0.0005. Gradient clipping at norm 1, bf16 mixed precision, and natural-prevalence sampling complete the configuration. Auxiliary loss weights remain fixed at 0.3, and the model comparisons use the same optimization budget.

Figure~\ref{fig:lrrange} shows the measured loss traces used to establish the learning-rate bracket, complementing the validation sweep in Fig.~\ref{fig:opt}.

\subsection{Neural Input-Recipe Comparison}
The seven six-epoch, seed-5052 trials in Fig.~\ref{fig:ablations} compare the available neural input recipes under a common optimizer. ART-only achieved AUROC 0.8833 and AUPRC 0.2620; ECG+ART achieved 0.8824 and 0.2588; and ECG+ART+PPG with SQI achieved 0.8802 and 0.2530. These results support ART as the mandatory pressure-bearing modality and PPG as an ablation rather than a cohort requirement. ECG is retained for beat alignment and the selected longer-horizon variability context.

All recipes in this comparison retain MAP-baseline context. The waveform-plane label identifies active image groups, while the physiological parameter study evaluates the scalar context separately. Together, the two comparisons establish the selected division between waveform encoding, physiological context, and quality-aware fusion.

\subsection{Implications for the Study Design}
The final design assigns complementary information to each branch. The 60-beat image describes waveform structure; one-minute parameters describe recent change; and five-minute parameters describe drift and instability. Within-signal morphology and SQI retain physiological shape and reliability without cross-signal delay shortcuts. The selected eight-feature context and AdamW configuration form the fixed specification for the BSHI experiments.

\section{Training and Generalization}
\label{sec:generalization}
\subsection{VitalDB-to-MOVER Transfer}
The source-only experiment fits network weights, feature imputation, scaling, and alarm thresholds using VitalDB training and validation patients only. The selected model is then evaluated on MOVER without updating weights or using target-site calibration. This measures transfer of the learned predictor under changed monitors and intermittent capture. A separate, explicitly labeled adapted analysis may recalibrate on a MOVER development subset, but it must not be mixed with the unadapted result.

Feature selection uses development data from both hospitals, while the source-only transfer fit uses VitalDB patients alone. This distinction separates the information used to design the representation from the data used to estimate network weights. Reported baseline test evaluations retain their original model and split definitions. Independent assessment uses a locked evaluation cohort; nested patient-level comparisons repeat data-dependent selection within the outer training folds.

\subsection{Pooled Patient-Independent Learning}
The pooled model learns from both hospitals, keeping every patient's surgeries and overlapping windows in one partition. Training patients determine imputation and scaling; validation patients determine early stopping and alarm thresholds. Evaluation reports pooled discrimination, hospital-specific AUROC and AUPRC, prevalence, and calibration. This protocol measures generalization to new patients at the two represented hospitals, complementing the source-only transfer experiment.

Natural-prevalence sampling is retained because patient and hospital balancing did not improve pooled discrimination in the feature comparison. Hospital-balanced sampling and class weighting are controlled sensitivity analyses, with evaluation at natural prevalence and calibration checked separately for each hospital.

\subsection{Controls Against Hospital Shortcuts}
Generalization is pursued through a common 100~Hz signal grid, consistent labels, physical ART scaling, within-window ECG/PPG normalization, causal features, and the exclusion of device-unit amplitudes and cross-signal delays from the primary model. Missing intervals remain masked. Domain classification is repeated for the joint input set, because low domain AUROC for each scalar does not guarantee low domain information in the full representation.

The primary core-cohort result keeps windows without PPG. The optional PPG comparison evaluates ECG+ART and ECG+ART+PPG on the same complete-modality windows, while the core-cohort result separately quantifies coverage. Missing-history, gray-zone, long-lead, and low-SQI subgroups are reported rather than removed. Controlled PPG dropout, ECG noise, ART damping/flatline, and timing perturbations test whether the quality gate responds usefully to corruption.

The selected configuration and principal context ablations use at least three seeds. Paired patient-level uncertainty compares the same evaluation windows. Alarm thresholds are selected on development data and frozen for dense replay. Calibration and alarm-threshold selection are evaluated as clinical operating-point decisions, while AUROC continues to measure score ranking.

\section{Big Data Component}
The harmonized cohorts contain approximately 14,800 waveform-hours, 52.7 million beats, and 1.16 million labeled windows; the three MOVER waveform archives occupy approximately 307~GB compressed. Scalable handling is consequently part of the engineering evaluation, not an additional clinical claim.

Selected tracks are processed in case/shard batches and stored in partitioned Parquet. A compact training store avoids reopening a different case file for every shuffled window; on the desktop it increased training throughput from approximately 60 to 150 windows/s on the on-demand path to approximately 2,200 windows/s. The selected parameter sidecar will join by window ID, preserving labels and splits without rebuilding waveform tensors. Partition hashes, feature schemas, normalization statistics, and run metadata will be versioned together.

PySpark supports parallel cohort assembly. Spark Structured Streaming replays time-ordered data, constructs rolling windows, and records predictions and latency. Benchmarks compare serial Python and Spark at increasing cohort fractions using wall-clock time, waveform-hours/s, windows/s, storage, and memory. Streaming reports p50/p95/p99 latency, backlog, throughput, and maximum sustainable replay speed. Local Spark measures multicore processing; the multi-worker configuration measures the additional scaling benefit.

\section{Contributions and Scope}
The project integrates a reproducible two-site cohort, a compact single-signal context specification, a quality-aware BSHI encoder, and direct forecasting of event risk and hypotension burden. Parameter selection is supported by matched pooled and transfer comparisons, while the optimization configuration is supported by a structured learning-rate and optimizer search. A versioned Parquet/Spark pipeline connects offline modeling to continuous replay and systems evaluation.

The primary scope is a five-minute horizon, ECG+ART input, eight engineered additions, and five MAP-baseline values. PPG is optional; EEG/BIS, ETCO$_2$, ten-minute prediction, and larger distributed experiments remain extensions. This is retrospective algorithm and systems research, not a clinical decision-support device.

\balance
\section{Risks and Mitigation}
The main risks are imperfect beat detection, intermittent long history, dominance of current MAP, zero-heavy burden targets, and hospital-specific shortcuts. The proposed controls are explicit masks, training-only preprocessing, a mandatory MAP comparator, two-part AUT65 regression, and matched patient-level comparisons. The selected scalar bundle is not universally complete in MOVER; short-history fallback and availability-flag ablations are required.

The main evaluation limitations are the invasive pressure-derived label, MOVER's smaller patient count, and differences in waveform capture. These are addressed by retaining MAP and ART-only comparators, reporting patient-cluster intervals, and separating complete-history and missing-history windows. Development-search intervals are interpreted jointly with AUPRC and hospital identifiability rather than as evidence from independent clinical trials. This keeps the conclusions tied to the discrimination, coverage, and transfer behavior actually measured.

\section{Conclusion}
The study selects ECG and ART waveforms, within-signal morphology and SQI, and eight independently extracted physiological parameters alongside a five-variable MAP baseline. In the gradient-boosting comparison, the selected context achieves validation AUROC 0.8817 pooled and 0.8058 for VitalDB-to-MOVER transfer, with substantially less hospital identifiability than the larger feature pool. AdamW with $\beta_2=0.95$, peak rate 0.0011, batch 128, and cosine decay provides the selected optimization configuration. The neural input comparison further supports retaining PPG as a matched ablation.

The resulting methodology combines beat-synchronous waveform encoding, multiscale physiological context, and reliability-aware fusion for event and burden forecasting. Source-only transfer evaluates robustness across acquisition systems, while pooled patient-independent learning evaluates performance across the combined population. Together with strong MAP comparators and measured streaming performance, these protocols define a coherent clinical-prediction and software-engineering study.
